On the Incompressible Limit of Current-Vortex Sheets with or without Surface Tension
Junyan Zhang

Abstract
This is the second part of the two-paper sequence, which aims to present a comprehensive study for current-vortex sheets in ideal compressible magnetohydrodynamics (MHD). The local well-posedness of current-vortex sheets with surface tension has been proved in the first part of the paper sequence [ 63 ] . In this paper, we prove the incompressible and zero-surface-tension limits under certain stability conditions. The proof of uniform estimates relies on the analysis of the evolution equation of the free interface via a paradifferential approach, the wave equation of the pressure and a weighted anisotropic structure in vorticity analysis.



Mathematics Subject Classification (2020):
76N10, 35Q35, 76W05, 35L65.

Keywords
: Current-vortex sheets, Magnetohydrodynamics, Incompressible limit, Kelvin-Helmholtz instability, Paradifferential calculus.

1
Introduction

Current-vortex sheet, as one of the characteristic discontinuities in ideal compressible MHD, is described by a free-interface problem of two-phase compressible ideal MHD flows where the magnetic fields are tangential to the interface and no mass flow transfers across the interface. This model has been widely used in both solar physics (e.g., the heliopause of the solar system, the night-side magnetopause of the earth)
[
8
]
and controlled nuclear fusion
[
21
]
. In particular, the surface tension effect on the interface cannot be neglected when the plasma is a liquid metal
[
41
]
.

Let
H>10
be a given real number,
x=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{d})
and
x^{\prime}:=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{d-1})
and the space dimension
d=2,3
. We define the regions
\Omega^{+}(t):=\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}:\psi(t,x^{\prime})<x_{d}<H\}
,
\Omega^{-}(t):=\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}:-H<x_{d}<\psi(t,x^{\prime})\}
and the moving interface
\Sigma(t):=\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}:x_{d}=\psi(t,x^{\prime})\}
between
\Omega^{+}(t)
and
\Omega^{-}(t)
. For
T>0
, we denote
\Omega_{T}^{\pm}:=\bigcup\limits_{0\leq t\leq T}\{t\}\times\Omega^{\pm}(t)
and
\Sigma_{T}:=\bigcup\limits_{0\leq t\leq T}\{t\}\times\Sigma(t)
. The following free-interface problem describes the motion of
\Sigma(t)
as a
current-vortex sheet
(or an
MHD tangential discontinuity
)

\begin{cases}\varrho^{\pm}(\partial_{t}+u^{\pm}\cdot\nabla)u^{\pm}-B^{\pm}\cdot\nabla B^{\pm}+\nabla Q^{\pm}=0,~~Q^{\pm}:=P^{\pm}+\frac{1}{2}|B^{\pm}|^{2}&~~\text{ in }\Omega_{T}^{\pm},\\
(\partial_{t}+u^{\pm}\cdot\nabla)\varrho^{\pm}+\varrho^{\pm}\nabla\cdot u^{\pm}=0&~~\text{ in }\Omega_{T}^{\pm},\\
(\partial_{t}+u^{\pm}\cdot\nabla)B^{\pm}=B^{\pm}\cdot\nabla u^{\pm}-B^{\pm}\nabla\cdot u^{\pm}&~~\text{ in }\Omega_{T}^{\pm},\\
\nabla\cdot B^{\pm}=0&~~\text{ in }\Omega_{T}^{\pm},\\
(\partial_{t}+u^{\pm}\cdot\nabla)\mathfrak{s}^{\pm}=0&~~\text{ in }\Omega_{T}^{\pm},\\
P^{\pm}=P^{\pm}(\varrho^{\pm},\mathfrak{s}^{\pm}),~~\frac{\partial P^{\pm}}{\partial\varrho^{\pm}}>0,~~\varrho^{\pm}\geq\overline{\rho}_{0}>0&~~\text{ in }\overline{\Omega_{T}^{\pm}},\\
\left\llbracket Q\right\rrbracket=\sigma\mathcal{H}&~~\text{ on }\Sigma_{T},\\
B^{\pm}\cdot N=0&~~\text{ on }\Sigma_{T},\\
\partial_{t}\psi=u^{\pm}\cdot N&~~\text{ on }\Sigma_{T},\\
u_{d}^{\pm}=B_{d}^{\pm}=0&~~\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Sigma^{\pm},\\
(u^{\pm},B^{\pm},\varrho^{\pm},\mathfrak{s}^{\pm})|_{t=0}=(u_{0}^{\pm},B_{0}^{\pm},\varrho_{0}^{\pm},\mathfrak{s}_{0}^{\pm})~~\text{ in }\Omega^{\pm}(0),\quad\quad\psi|_{t=0}=\psi_{0}&~~\text{ on }\Sigma(0).\end{cases}

(1.1)

Here
\nabla:=(\partial_{x_{1}},\cdots,\partial_{x_{d}})
is the standard spatial derivative.
D_{t}:=\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla
is the material derivative. The fluid velocity, the magnetic field, the fluid density, the fluid pressure and the entropy are denoted by
u=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{d})
,
B=(B_{1},\cdots,B_{d})
,
\varrho
,
P
and
\mathfrak{s}
respectively. The quantity
Q:=P+\frac{1}{2}|B|^{2}
is the total pressure. Note that the fourth equation in (
1.1
) is just an initial constraint instead of an independent equation. The fifth equation of (
1.1
) is derived from the equation of total energy and Gibbs relation and we refer to
[
21
, Ch. 4.3]
for more details. To close system (
1.1
), we need to introduce the equation of state

P=P(\varrho,\mathfrak{s})\text{ satisfying }\frac{\partial P}{\partial\varrho}>0.

(1.2)

The assumption
\varrho\geq\bar{\rho_{0}}>0
(for some constant
\bar{\rho_{0}}>0
) together with
\frac{\partial P}{\partial\varrho}>0
guarantees the hyperbolicity of system (
1.1
).

The seventh to the tenth equations in (
1.1
) are the boundary conditions, in which
N:=(-\partial_{1}\psi,\cdots,-\partial_{d-1}\psi,1)^{\top}
is the normal vector to
\Sigma(t)
(pointing towards
\Omega^{+}(t)
),
\sigma\geq 0
is the constant coefficient of surface tension and the quantity
\mathcal{H}:=\overline{\nabla}\cdot\left(\frac{\overline{\nabla}\psi}{\sqrt{1+|\overline{\nabla}\psi|^{2}}}\right)
is twice the mean curvature of
\Sigma(t)
with
\overline{\nabla}=(\partial_{1},\cdots,\partial_{d-1})
. The jump of a function
f
on
\Sigma(t)
is denoted by
\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket:=f^{+}|_{\Sigma(t)}-f^{-}|_{\Sigma(t)}
with
f^{\pm}:=f|_{\Omega^{\pm}(t)}
.

•

The first boundary condition
\left\llbracket Q\right\rrbracket=\sigma\mathcal{H}
shows that the jump of total pressure is balanced by surface tension.

•

The second boundary condition
B^{\pm}\cdot N=0
shows that both plasmas are perfect conductors.

•

The third boundary condition
\partial_{t}\psi=u^{\pm}\cdot N
shows that there is no mass flow across the interface and thus the two plasmas are physically contact and mutually impermeable.

•

The slip boundary conditions are imposed on the rigid boundaries
\Sigma^{\pm}:=\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\times\{\pm H\}
.

We refer to
[
54
, Appendix A]
for detailed derivation of these boundary conditions from the Rankine-Hugoniot conditions. Also note that the conditions
\nabla\cdot B^{\pm}=0
in
\Omega^{\pm}(t)
,
B^{\pm}\cdot N|_{\Sigma(t)}=0
and
B^{\pm}_{d}=0
on
\Sigma^{\pm}
are constraints for initial data so that system (
1.1
) is not over-determined. These initial constraints can propagate within the lifespan of solutions.

We also require the initial data to satisfy certain compatibility conditions. Let
(u_{0}^{\pm},B_{0}^{\pm},\varrho_{0}^{\pm},\mathfrak{s}_{0}^{\pm},\psi_{0}):=(u^{\pm},B^{\pm},\varrho^{\pm},\mathfrak{s}^{\pm},\psi)|_{t=0}
be the initial data of system (
1.1
). We say the initial data satisfies the compatibility condition up to
m
-th order
(m\in\mathbb{N})
if

\displaystyle(D_{t}^{\pm})^{j}\left\llbracket Q\right\rrbracket|_{t=0}=\sigma(D_{t}^{\pm})^{j}\mathcal{H}|_{t=0}~~\text{ on }\Sigma(0),~~0\leq j\leq m,

(1.3)

\displaystyle(D_{t}^{\pm})^{j}\partial_{t}\psi|_{t=0}=(D_{t}^{\pm})^{j}(u^{\pm}\cdot N)|_{t=0}~~\text{ on }\Sigma(0),~~0\leq j\leq m,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{j}u_{d}^{\pm}=0~~\text{ on }\Sigma^{\pm},~~0\leq j\leq m.

In fact, the fulfillment of the first condition implicitly requires the fulfillment of the second one. With these compatibility conditions, one can show that the magnetic fields also satisfy (cf.
[
53
, Section 4.1]
)

(D_{t}^{\pm})^{j}(B^{\pm}\cdot N)|_{t=0}=0~~\text{ on }\Sigma(0)\text{ and }\Sigma^{\pm},~~0\leq j\leq m.

System (
1.1
) admits a conserved
L^{2}
energy

\displaystyle E_{0}(t):=\sum_{\pm}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega^{\pm}(t)}\varrho^{\pm}|u^{\pm}|^{2}+|B^{\pm}|^{2}+2\mathfrak{P}(\varrho^{\pm},\mathfrak{s}^{\pm})+\varrho^{\pm}|\mathfrak{s}^{\pm}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\sigma\text{ Area}(\Sigma(t))

where
\mathfrak{P}(\varrho^{\pm},\mathfrak{s}^{\pm})=\int_{\bar{\rho}_{0}}^{\varrho^{\pm}}\frac{P^{\pm}(z,\mathfrak{s}^{\pm})}{z^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}z
. See the first part of this paper sequence
[
63
, Section 3.1]
for the proof.

The local well-posedness and the incompressible limit of (
1.1
) for each fixed
\sigma>0
has been established in
[
63
, Section 3.1]
. This paper is a continuation of
[
63
]
: we aim to prove the zero-surface-tension limit for the solution to (
1.1
) under certain stability conditions and improve the low Mach number limit result in
[
63
]
such that the uniform boundedness (with respect to the Mach number) of high-order (
\geq 2
order) time derivatives can be dropped. The motivation to study these limit processes are stated in Section
1.3
.

1.1
Reformulation in flattened domains

1.1.1
Flattening the fluid domains

As in
[
63
]
, we convert (
1.1
) into a PDE system defined in
\Omega^{\pm}:=\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\times\{0<\pm x_{d}<H\}.
We consider a family of diffeomorphisms
\Phi(t,\cdot):\Omega^{\pm}\to\Omega^{\pm}(t)
characterized by the moving interface. Let
\Phi(t,x^{\prime},x_{d})=\left(x^{\prime},\varphi(t,x_{d})\right)

where

\displaystyle\varphi(t,x)=x_{d}+\chi(x_{d})\psi(t,x^{\prime})

(1.4)

and
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([-H,H])
is a smooth cut-off function satisfying the following bounds:

{\|\chi^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\frac{1}{\|\psi_{0}\|_{\infty}+20},\quad\sum_{j=1}^{8}\|\chi^{(j)}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C,}\quad\chi=1\,\,\,\,\text{on}\,\,(-1,1)

(1.5)

for some generic constant
C>0
. We assume
|\psi_{0}|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\leq 1
. One can prove that there exists some
T_{0}>0
such that
\sup\limits_{[0,T_{0}]}|\psi(t,\cdot)|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}<10<H
, the free interface is still a graph within the time interval
[0,T_{0}]
and

\partial_{d}\varphi(t,x^{\prime},x_{d})=1+\chi^{\prime}(x_{d})\psi(t,x^{\prime})=1-\frac{1}{20}\times 10\geq\frac{1}{2},\quad t\in[0,T_{0}],

which ensures that
\Phi(t)
is a diffeomorphism in
[0,T_{0}]
. We then introduce the new variables

\displaystyle v^{\pm}(t,x)=u^{\pm}(t,\Phi(t,x)),\quad b^{\pm}(t,x)=B^{\pm}(t,\Phi(t,x)),\quad\rho^{\pm}(t,x)=\varrho^{\pm}(t,\Phi(t,x)),

\displaystyle S^{\pm}(t,x)=\mathfrak{s}^{\pm}(t,\Phi(t,x)),\quad q^{\pm}(t,x)=Q^{\pm}(t,\Phi(t,x)),\quad p^{\pm}(t,x)=P(t,\Phi(t,x))

(1.6)

that represent the velocity fields, the magnetic fields, the density functions, the entropy functions, the total pressure functions and the fluid pressure functions defined in the fixed domains
\Omega^{\pm}
respectively. Also, we introduce the differential operators

\displaystyle\nabla^{\varphi}=(\partial^{\varphi}_{1},\cdots,\partial^{\varphi}_{d}),~\text{where }

\displaystyle~\partial^{\varphi}_{a}=\partial_{a}-\frac{\partial_{a}\varphi}{\partial_{d}\varphi}\partial_{d},~~a=t,1,\cdots,d-1;~~\partial_{d}^{\varphi}=\frac{1}{\partial_{d}\varphi}\partial_{d}.

(1.7)

We set the tangential gradient operator and the tangential derivatives as
\overline{\nabla}:=(\partial_{1},\cdots,\partial_{d-1}),~\overline{\partial}{}_{i}:=\partial_{i},~i=1,\cdots,d-1.
Invoking (
1.7
), we can alternatively write the material derivative
D_{t}^{\varphi}
as

D_{t}^{\varphi\pm}=\partial_{t}+\bar{v}^{\pm}\cdot\overline{\nabla}+\frac{1}{\partial_{d}\varphi}(v^{\pm}\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi)\partial_{d},

(1.8)

where
\bar{v}^{\pm}:=(v_{1}^{\pm},\cdots,v_{d-1}^{\pm})^{\top}
consists of the horizontal components of the fluid velocity and

\bar{v}^{\pm}\cdot\overline{\nabla}:=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d-1}v_{j}^{\pm}\partial_{j}
.
\mathbf{N}:=(-\partial_{1}\varphi,\cdots,-\partial_{d-1}\varphi,1)^{\top}
is the extension of the normal vector
N
into
\Omega^{\pm}
. This formulation will be helpful for us to simplify the analysis.

1.1.2
Parametrization of the equation of state

We assume the fluids in
\Omega^{+}
and
\Omega^{-}
satisfy the same equation of state. Specifically, we parametrize the equation of state to be
\rho_{\lambda}(p,S):=\rho(p/\lambda^{2},S)
where
\lambda>0
is proportional to the sound speed
c_{s}:=\sqrt{\partial_{p}\rho}
and
\rho
is a
C^{8}
function in its arguments satisfying
\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial p}>0
as well as the non-degeneracy condition
\rho\geq\bar{\rho_{0}}>0
in
\overline{\Omega}
for some constant
\bar{\rho_{0}}
. By chain rule, it is straightforward to see

\displaystyle 0<\frac{\partial}{\partial p}\rho_{\lambda}(p,S)\leq C\lambda^{-2}.

(1.9)

and

\displaystyle|(\partial_{p})^{k}\rho_{\lambda}(p,S)|\leq C\lambda^{-2k},\quad\quad|(\partial_{S})^{k}\rho_{\lambda}(p,S)|\leq C,\quad\quad 1\leq k\leq 8,

(1.10)

for some
C>0.
For example, a polytropic gas satisfies the above assumptions whose the equation of state is parametrized in terms of
\lambda>0
:

p_{\lambda}(\rho,S)=\lambda^{2}\left(\rho^{\gamma}\exp(S/C_{V})-1\right),~~~\gamma>1,~~C_{V}>0.

(1.11)

The formulation used in this manuscript.

For sake of clean notations, we would introduce the quantity
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}:=\log\rho^{\pm}
to replace
\rho
and introduce the parameter
\varepsilon:=1/\lambda
to replace
\lambda
in the continuity equation, that is,
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(p,S):=\log\rho_{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}(p,S)
. Since
\frac{\partial p^{\pm}}{\partial\rho^{\pm}}>0
and
\rho^{\pm}>0
imply
\frac{\partial\mathcal{F}^{\pm}}{\partial p^{\pm}}=\frac{1}{\rho^{\pm}}\frac{\partial\rho^{\pm}}{\partial p^{\pm}}>0
, then the continuity equation is equivalent to

\frac{\partial\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}{\partial p^{\pm}}(p^{\pm},S^{\pm})D_{t}^{\varphi\pm}p^{\pm}+\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot v^{\pm}=0.

(1.12)

(
1.9
)-(
1.10
) lead to the following inequalities: There exists a constant
A>0
such that

\displaystyle 0<\frac{\partial\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}}{\partial p}(p,S)\leq

\displaystyle~A\varepsilon^{2},

(1.13)

\displaystyle|\partial_{p}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(p,S)|\leq A\varepsilon^{2k},~~~|\partial_{S}^{k}\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(p,S)|\leq

\displaystyle~A,\quad\quad 1\leq k\leq 8.

(1.14)

In what follows, we slightly abuse the terminology and call
\lambda
the sound speed and call
\varepsilon
the Mach number. When discussing the incompressible limit (
\lambda\gg 1
or equivalently
\varepsilon\ll 1
), we sometimes write
\mathcal{F}_{p}^{\pm}:=\frac{\partial\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}}{\partial p}(p^{\pm},S^{\pm})=\varepsilon^{2}
for simplicity.

System (
1.1
) is now converted into

\begin{cases}\rho^{\pm}D_{t}^{\varphi\pm}v^{\pm}-(b^{\pm}\cdot\nabla^{\varphi})b^{\pm}+\nabla^{\varphi}q^{\pm}=0,~~q^{\pm}=p^{\pm}+\frac{1}{2}|b^{\pm}|^{2}&~~\text{ in }[0,T]\times\Omega^{\pm},\\
\mathcal{F}_{p}^{\pm}D_{t}^{\varphi\pm}p^{\pm}+\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot v^{\pm}=0&~~\text{ in }[0,T]\times\Omega^{\pm},\\
p^{\pm}=p^{\pm}(\rho^{\pm},S^{\pm}),~~\mathcal{F}^{\pm}=\log\rho^{\pm},~~\mathcal{F}_{p}^{\pm}>0,~\rho^{\pm}\geq\bar{\rho_{0}}>0&~~\text{ in }[0,T]\times\Omega^{\pm},\\
D_{t}^{\varphi\pm}b^{\pm}-(b^{\pm}\cdot\nabla^{\varphi})v^{\pm}+b^{\pm}\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot v^{\pm}=0&~~\text{ in }[0,T]\times\Omega^{\pm},\\
\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot b^{\pm}=0&~~\text{ in }[0,T]\times\Omega^{\pm},\\
D_{t}^{\varphi\pm}S^{\pm}=0&~~\text{ in }[0,T]\times\Omega^{\pm},\\
\left\llbracket q\right\rrbracket=\sigma\overline{\nabla}\cdot\left(\frac{\overline{\nabla}\psi}{\sqrt{1+|\overline{\nabla}\psi|^{2}}}\right)&~~\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Sigma,\\
\partial_{t}\psi=v^{\pm}\cdot N&~~\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Sigma,\\
b^{\pm}\cdot N=0&~~\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Sigma,\\
v_{d}^{\pm}=b_{d}^{\pm}=0&~~\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Sigma^{\pm},\\
{(v^{\pm},b^{\pm},\rho^{\pm},S^{\pm},\psi)|_{t=0}=(v_{0}^{\pm},b_{0}^{\pm},\rho_{0}^{\pm},S_{0}^{\pm},\psi_{0})}.\end{cases}

(1.15)

Since the material derivatives are tangential to the boundary, that is,
D_{t}^{\varphi\pm}=\overline{D_{t}^{\pm}}:=\partial_{t}+\bar{v}^{\pm}\cdot\overline{\nabla}
on
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\pm}
, the compatibility conditions (
1.3
) for initial data up to
m
-th order
(m\in\mathbb{N})
are now written as:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\partial_{t}^{j}q\right\rrbracket|_{t=0}=\sigma\partial_{t}^{j}\mathcal{H}|_{t=0}~~\text{ on }\Sigma,~~0\leq j\leq m,

(1.16)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{j+1}\psi|_{t=0}=\partial_{t}^{j}(v^{\pm}\cdot N)|_{t=0}~~\text{ on }\Sigma,~~0\leq j\leq m,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{j}v_{d}^{\pm}|_{t=0}=0~~\text{ on }\Sigma^{\pm},~~0\leq j\leq m.

The above conditions also imply
\partial_{t}^{j}(b^{\pm}\cdot N)|_{t=0}=0
on
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\pm}
for
0\leq j\leq m
. See
[
53
, Section 4]
for details.

1.1.3
Stability conditions for the zero-surface-tension limit

We need to add some extra stability conditions on the free interface when surface tension is neglected, that is, when
\sigma=0
. We introduce the quantities

a^{\pm}:=\sqrt{\rho^{\pm}\left(1+\left(\frac{c_{A}^{\pm}}{c_{s}^{\pm}}\right)^{2}\right)}

where
c_{A}^{\pm}:=|b^{\pm}|/\sqrt{\rho^{\pm}}
represents the Alfvén speed (the speed of magneto-sonic waves),
c_{s}^{\pm}:=\sqrt{\partial p^{\pm}/\partial\rho^{\pm}}
represents the sound speed. The stability conditions are

\displaystyle d=3:

\displaystyle~~0<a^{\pm}\left|\bar{b}^{\mp}\times\left\llbracket\bar{v}\right\rrbracket\right|<|\bar{b}^{+}\times\bar{b}^{-}|~~\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Sigma,

(1.17)

\displaystyle d=2:

\displaystyle~~\left(\frac{|b_{1}^{+}|}{a^{+}}+\frac{|b_{1}^{-}|}{a^{-}}\right)>|\left\llbracket v_{1}\right\rrbracket|>0~~\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Sigma,

(1.18)

where we view the horizontal magnetic field
\bar{b}=(b_{1},b_{2},0)^{\top}
and the horizontal velocity
\bar{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},0)^{\top}
as vectors lying on
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\{x_{3}=0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
to define the exterior product. The “
>0
” part in (
1.17
) and (
1.18
) is necessary because vortex sheets naturally require the tangential discontinuity of velocity is nonzero. Thus, the stability conditions require that the strength of the magnetic fields cannot be too weak. Moreover, the condition for 3D case implies that
b^{+}
and
b^{-}
are not collinear on
\Sigma
and the condition for 2D case requires certain quantitative relation between the strength of magnetic fields and the jump of tangential velocities. Note that the stability conditions are just initial constraints that can propagate within a short time interval. We will explain in later sections why such stability conditions are needed.

1.2
An overview of previous results

For incompressible Euler equations, vortex sheets without surface tension tend to be violently unstable, which exhibit the so-called Kelvin-Helmholtz instability. We refer to
[
18
,
58
,
59
]
and references therein for the special case of 2D irrotational flows without surface tension and
[
6
,
12
,
46
]
for the case of nonzero vorticity and nonzero surface tension. For compressible Euler equations, vortex sheets, unlike shock fronts, are characteristic discontinuities and the uniform Kreiss-Lopatinskiĭ condition is never satisfied, which leads to a potential loss of normal derivatives. For 3D Euler equations, compressible vortex sheets exhibit an analogue of Kelvin-Helmholtz instability
[
19
,
40
,
51
]
; whereas for 2D Euler equations, Coulombel-Secchi
[
14
,
15
]
proved the existence of “supersonic” vortex sheets when the Mach number for the rectilinear background solution exceeds
\sqrt{2}
, which is also a critical Mach number for the neutral stability. For the case of nonzero surface tension, we refer to Stevens
[
47
]
for the local existence.

The Kelvin-Helmholtz instability can also be suppressed by magnetic fields. Unlike the transversal magnetic fields in MHD contact discontinuities (
B\cdot N|_{\Sigma(t)}\neq 0
)
[
57
]
, the tangential magnetic fields must satisfy some constraints when the surface tension is neglected. For 3D incompressible ideal MHD, the following Syrovatskiĭ condition
[
50
]
is assumed for local well-posedness

\varrho^{+}|B^{+}\times\left\llbracket u\right\rrbracket|^{2}+\varrho^{-}|B^{-}\times\left\llbracket u\right\rrbracket|^{2}<(\varrho^{+}+\varrho^{-})|B^{+}\times B^{-}|^{2}.

(1.19)

Coulombel-Morando-Secchi-Trebeschi
[
13
]
proved the a priori estimate for the nonlinear problem under a more restrictive condition

\max\left\{\left|\frac{B^{+}}{\sqrt{\varrho^{+}}}\times\left\llbracket u\right\rrbracket\right|,\left|\frac{B^{-}}{\sqrt{\varrho^{-}}}\times\left\llbracket u\right\rrbracket\right|\right\}<\left|\frac{B^{+}}{\sqrt{\varrho^{+}}}\times\frac{B^{-}}{\sqrt{\varrho^{-}}}\right|.

(1.20)

Sun-Wang-Zhang
[
48
]
proved local well-posedness of the nonlinear problem under the original Syrovatskiĭ condition (
1.19
). See also Liu-Xin
[
34
]
and Li-Li
[
31
]
for the study for both
\sigma>0
and
\sigma=0
cases.

For compressible current-vortex sheets in ideal MHD, it is unclear whether there is any
necessary and sufficient condition
for the linear stability when the surface tension is neglected. Trakhinin
[
52
]
introduced a sufficient condition for the problem linearized around a background planar current-vortex sheet
(\hat{v}^{\pm},\hat{b}^{\pm},\hat{\rho}^{\pm},\hat{S}^{\pm})
in flattened domains
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{\pm}
, which reads

|\left\llbracket\hat{v}\right\rrbracket|<|\sin(\bm{\alpha}^{+}-\bm{\alpha}^{-})|\min\left\{\frac{\bm{\gamma}^{+}}{|\sin\bm{\alpha}^{-}|},\frac{\bm{\gamma}^{-}}{|\sin\bm{\alpha}^{+}|}\right\}\quad\text{ on }\{x_{3}=0\},

(1.21)

where
\bm{\gamma}^{\pm}:=\frac{c_{A}^{\pm}}{\sqrt{1+({c_{A}^{\pm}}/{c_{s}^{\pm}})^{2}}}
,
c_{A}^{\pm}:=|\hat{b}^{\pm}|/\sqrt{\hat{\rho}^{\pm}}
represents the Alfvén speed,
c_{s}^{\pm}:=\sqrt{\partial\hat{p}^{\pm}/\partial\hat{\rho}^{\pm}}
represents the sound speed, and
\bm{\alpha}^{\pm}
represents the oriented angle between
\left\llbracket\hat{v}\right\rrbracket
and
\hat{b}^{\pm}
. Indeed, (
1.21
) is equivalent to (
1.17
) for the constant-coefficient linearized problem. Also note that the formal incompressible limit of this condition as
\hat{\rho}^{\pm}\to 1
is exactly (
1.20
), and it is easy to see (
1.20
) implies (
1.19
). The well-posedness was proven by Chen-Wang
[
10
]
under a more restrictive condition and Trakhinin
[
53
]
under (
1.17
) with the help of Nash-Moser iteration. See also
[
42
]
for the 2D problem without surface tension under the stability condition (
1.18
).

However, the study of compressible current-vortex sheets with surface tension is unavailable prior to the first part
[
63
]
of this two-paper sequence. Also, the local existence results in previous works about free-surface compressible ideal MHD were all established by Nash-Moser iteration. There were quite few results about the energy estimates without loss of regularity: To the author’s knowledge, the author’s work
[
33
]
presents the only available result (except
[
63
]
) about the energy estimates without loss of regularity for one-phase MHD without surface tension, but the energy estimates in
[
33
]
are never uniform in Mach number.
The study of singular limits (e.g., the incompressible limit) of both inviscid vortex sheets and the free-surface ideal MHD flows remains open
.

1.3
Our goals

We aim to present a comprehensive study of current-vortex sheets in ideal MHD and to develop a systematic approach to study the well-posedness and the singular limits for compressible inviscid fluids. The local well-posedness and the incompressible limit of (
1.1
) for each fixed
\sigma>0
has been established in
[
63
]
. In this paper, we aim the prove the following results:

•

The incompressible and zero-surface-tension limits under the stability conditions (
1.17
) or (
1.18
) in 3D or 2D respectively. It should be noted that these two limit process are independent of each other, that is, the energy estimates are uniform in both Mach number and
\sigma
under (
1.17
) or (
1.18
).

•

The improved incompressible limit such that the uniform boundedness (with respect to Mach number) of high-order (
\geq 2
) time derivatives of velocity fields can be dropped.

To our knowledge, this paper presents
the first
result about the incompressible limit for both inviscid vortex sheets and free-surface MHD flows.

The study of singular limits of magneto-fluids has been a long-term research topic as stated in Majda’s book
[
37
, Section 2.4]
. For ideal compressible MHD, the singular-limit theory (e.g., the low Mach number limit, the small Alfvén number limit), which has shown great importance in the modelling of nuclear fusion or solar winds, is far less developed even for the fixed-domain problems, especially when the magnetic fields are tangential to the boundary. One of the major difficulty under the low Mach number setting is the
“mismatch” of function spaces
for the local existence: The linearized problem of compressible ideal MHD in a fixed domain with
B\cdot N=0
on the boundary is ill-posed in standard Sobolev spaces
H^{l}(l\geq 2)
(see
[
43
]
), whereas the incompressible problem is well-posed in standard Sobolev spaces (e.g., see
[
22
]
). The anisotropic Sobolev spaces defined in Section
1.4.1
, introduced by Shu-Xing Chen
[
11
]
, were adopted in previous works about ideal compressible MHD (e.g.,
[
60
,
53
,
10
,
33
]
), but none of the energy estimates obtained in these works is uniform in Mach number. The above issues are not resolved until the appearance of the author’s recent preparatory work
[
55
]
(joint with J. Wang, for the fixed-domain problem in the reference domain
\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\times(-1,1)
) and this paper sequence.

When the fluid domain is
\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{T}^{d}
or a domain with a fixed boundary, there have been numerous results concerning the low Mach number limit of compressible inviscid fluids (e.g., the pioneering works
[
28
,
17
,
44
,
39
,
1
]
about Euler equations), including the case of “ill-prepared (general) data” (
\text{div }v_{0}=O(1),~\partial_{t}v|_{t=0}=O(1/\varepsilon)
). However, for free-boundary problems, the study of singular limits remains largely open: There is even no robust framework to establish the low Mach number limit for inviscid fluids with “well-prepared data” (
\text{div }v_{0}=O(\varepsilon),~\partial_{t}v|_{t=0}=O(1)
). The results in previous works
[
32
,
35
,
16
,
61
,
36
]
and the first part
[
63
]
of this paper sequence require the uniform boundedness with respect to Mach number up to
top-order
time derivatives. In the author’s previous work
[
62
]
, the boundedness assumption on high-order
(\geq 3)
time derivatives
1
1

1

When
\sigma=0
, the Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition
-\nabla_{N}q|_{\Sigma(t)}>0
is necessary for the local existence. To propagate the Rayleigh-Taylor sign, one has to require
\partial_{t}q\sim\partial_{t}^{2}v
to be uniformly bounded. Very recently, Gu-Wang
[
23
]
derived the
L_{t}^{2}
-type bound of
\partial_{t}q
for free-surface Euler equations with heat conduction, but this is completely contributied by the parabolic feature of the heat-conductive part.
was dropped for one-phase elastodynamic flows
without
surface tension (also applicable to Euler equations), in which the framework is not applicable to two-phase fluids or fluids with surface tension.

The zero-surface-tension limit, despite not a singular limit, rigorously shows that either surface tension or suitable magnetic field brings suppression effect on the analogue of Kelvin-Helmholtz instability for compressible vortex sheets. Also, in Section
2.1
, we will see the comparison between the stabilization mechanism brought by surface tension and the one brought by magnetic fields satisfying (
1.17
) or (
1.18
).

In this paper, we developed
a different framework (also applicable to Euler equations) that can help us drop the boundedness assumption of high-order time derivatives regardless of surface tension
. This framework is also applicable to compressible vortex-sheet problems if the two-phase fluids (not only for MHD, but also for Euler equations) are isentropic and the density functions are approaching the same constant as the Mach number goes to 0. In Section
2.2.2
and Section
2.2.3
, we will see this method is the combination of a paradifferential approach applied to the evolution equation of the free interface and the analysis of wave equations for the pressure functions in
\varepsilon^{2}
-weighted Sobolev spaces.

1.4
Main results

1.4.1
Anisotropic Sobolev spaces

Following the notations in
[
11
,
55
]
, we first define the anisotropic Sobolev space
H_{*}^{m}(\Omega^{\pm})
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\Omega^{\pm}=\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\times\{0<\pm x_{d}<H\}
. Let
\omega=\omega(x_{d})=(H^{2}-x_{d}^{2})x_{d}^{2}
be a smooth function on
[-H,H]
. The choice of
\omega(x_{d})
is not unique, as we just need
\omega(x_{d})
vanishes on
\Sigma\cup\Sigma^{\pm}
and is comparable to the distance function near the interface and the boundaries. Then we define
H_{*}^{m}(\Omega^{\pm})
for
m\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
as follows

H_{*}^{m}(\Omega^{\pm}):=\left\{f\in L^{2}(\Omega^{\pm})\bigg|(\omega\partial_{d})^{\alpha_{d+1}}\partial_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial_{d}^{\alpha_{d}}f\in L^{2}(\Omega^{\pm}),~~\forall\alpha\text{ with }\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}\alpha_{j}+2\alpha_{d}+\alpha_{d+1}\leq m\right\},

equipped with the norm

\|f\|_{H_{*}^{m}(\Omega^{\pm})}^{2}:=\sum_{\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d-1}\alpha_{j}+2\alpha_{d}+\alpha_{d+1}\leq m}\|(\omega\partial_{d})^{\alpha_{d+1}}\partial_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial_{d}^{\alpha_{d}}f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}.

(1.22)

For any multi-index
\alpha:=(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{d},\alpha_{d+1})\in\mathbb{N}^{d+2}
, we define

\partial_{*}^{\alpha}:=\partial_{t}^{\alpha_{0}}(\omega\partial_{d})^{\alpha_{d+1}}\partial_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial_{d}^{\alpha_{d}},~~\langle\alpha\rangle:=\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\alpha_{j}+2\alpha_{d}+\alpha_{d+1},

and define the
space-time anisotropic Sobolev norm

\|\cdot\|_{m,*,\pm}
to be

\|f\|_{m,*,\pm}^{2}:=\sum_{\langle\alpha\rangle\leq m}\|\partial_{*}^{\alpha}f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\pm})}^{2}=\sum_{\alpha_{0}\leq m}\|\partial_{t}^{\alpha_{0}}f\|_{H_{*}^{m-\alpha_{0}}(\Omega^{\pm})}^{2}.

(1.23)

We also write the interior Sobolev norm to be
\|f\|_{s,\pm}:=\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{s}(\Omega^{\pm})}
for any function
f(t,x)\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Omega^{\pm}
and denote the boundary Sobolev norm to be
|f|_{s}:=|f(t,\cdot)|_{H^{s}(\Sigma)}
for any function
f(t,x^{\prime})\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Sigma
.

From now on, we assume the dimension
d=3
, that is,
\Omega^{\pm}=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\{0<\pm x_{3}<H\}
,
\Sigma^{\pm}=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\{x_{3}=\pm H\}
and
\Sigma=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\{x_{3}=0\}
. We will see the 2D case follows in the same manner as the 3D case up to slight modifications in the vorticity analysis and the analysis of stability condition when
\sigma=0
.

1.4.2
The existence theorem for compressible current-vortex sheets with surface tension

Let us first record a theorem in
[
63
]
about well-posedness and uniform-in-
\varepsilon
estimates of (
1.15
) for a fixed
\sigma>0
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Fix the constant
\sigma>0
. Let
v_{0}^{\pm},b_{0}^{\pm},\rho_{0}^{\pm},S_{0}^{\pm}\in H_{*}^{8}(\Omega^{\pm})
and
\psi_{0}\in H^{9.5}(\Sigma)
be the initial data of (
1.15
) satisfying

•

the compatibility conditions (
1.16
) up to 7-th order;

•

the constraints
\nabla^{\varphi_{0}}\cdot b_{0}^{\pm}=0
in
\Omega^{\pm}
,
b^{\pm}\cdot N|_{\{t=0\}\times(\Sigma\cup\Sigma^{\pm})}=0
;

•

|\left\llbracket\bar{v}_{0}\right\rrbracket|>0
on
\Sigma
,
|\psi_{0}|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\leq 1
, and
E(0)\leq M
for some constant
M>0
.

Then there exists
T_{\sigma}>0
depending only on
M
and
\sigma
, such that (
1.15
) admits a unique solution
(v^{\pm}(t),b^{\pm}(t),\rho^{\pm}(t),S^{\pm}(t),\psi(t))
verifies the energy estimate

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}E(t)\leq C(\sigma^{-1})P(E(0))

(1.24)

and
\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T_{\sigma}]}|\psi(t)|<10<H
, where
P(\cdots)
is a generic polynomial in its arguments. The energy
E(t)
is defined to be

\displaystyle E(t):=

\displaystyle E_{4}(t)+E_{5}(t)+E_{6}(t)+E_{7}(t)+E_{8}(t),

(1.25)

\displaystyle E_{4+l}(t):=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pm}\sum_{\langle\alpha\rangle=2l}\sum_{k=0}^{4-l}\left\|\left(\varepsilon^{2l}\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{k}\left(v^{\pm},b^{\pm},S^{\pm},(\mathcal{F}_{p}^{\pm})^{\frac{(k+\alpha_{0}-l-3)_{+}}{2}}p^{\pm}\right)\right)\right\|^{2}_{4-k-l,\pm}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=0}^{4+l}\left|\sqrt{\sigma}\varepsilon^{2l}\partial_{t}^{k}\psi\right|^{2}_{5+l-k}\quad 0\leq l\leq 4,

where
k_{+}:=\max\{k,0\}
for
k\in\mathbb{R}
and we denote
\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}:=(\omega(x_{3})\partial_{3})^{\alpha_{4}}\partial_{t}^{\alpha_{0}}\partial_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}
to be a high-order tangential derivative for the multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},0,\alpha_{4})
with length (for the anisotropic Sobolev spaces)
\langle\alpha\rangle=\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2\times 0+\alpha_{4}
. The quantity
\varepsilon
is the parameter defined in Section
1.1.2
. Moreover, the
H^{9.5}(\Sigma)
-regularity of
\psi
can be recovered in the sense that

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{4}\sum_{k=0}^{3+l}\left|\sigma\varepsilon^{2l}\partial_{t}^{k}\psi\right|^{2}_{5.5+l-k}\leq P(E(t)),\quad\forall t\in[0,T_{\sigma}].

(1.26)

1.4.3
Main result 1: Incompressible and zero-surface-tension limits

For any fixed
\sigma>0
, the energy estimates obtained in Theorem
1.1
are already uniform in
\varepsilon
. When taking the limit
\sigma\to 0
, we shall impose suitable stability conditions on
\Sigma
to ensure the well-posedness of “
\sigma=0
-problem”. Assume there exists a constant
\delta_{0}\in(0,\frac{1}{8})
such that

\displaystyle\delta_{0}\leq a^{\pm}\left|\bar{b}^{\mp}\times\left\llbracket\bar{v}\right\rrbracket\right|\leq(1-\delta_{0})|\bar{b}^{+}\times\bar{b}^{-}|~~\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Sigma,

(1.27)

where

\displaystyle a^{\pm}:=\sqrt{\rho^{\pm}\left(1+\left(\frac{c_{A}^{\pm}}{c_{s}^{\pm}}\right)^{2}\right)}

(1.28)

and
c_{A}^{\pm}:=|b^{\pm}|/\sqrt{\rho^{\pm}}
represents the Alfvén speed (the speed of magnetosonic wave),
c_{s}^{\pm}:=\sqrt{\partial p^{\pm}/\partial\rho^{\pm}}
represents the sound speed. It should be noted that (
1.27
) is not an imposed boundary condition for the “
\sigma=0
”-problem. Instead, it is just a constraint for initial data which can propagate within a short time. In other words, we only need to assume

\displaystyle 2\delta_{0}\leq(a^{\pm}|_{t=0})\left|\bar{b}_{0}^{\mp}\times\left\llbracket\bar{v}_{0}\right\rrbracket\right|\leq(1-2\delta_{0})|\bar{b}_{0}^{+}\times\bar{b}_{0}^{-}|~~\text{ on }\Sigma.

(1.29)

Under the stability condition (
1.27
), we can establish the uniform-in-
({\varepsilon,\sigma})
energy estimates.

Theorem 1.2

(
Uniform-in-
({\varepsilon,\sigma})
estimates
)
.

Under the hypothesis of Theorem
1.1
, if the stability condition (
1.27
) holds, then there exists a time
T>0
only depending on
M
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\widetilde{E}(t)\leq P(\widetilde{E}(0)),

(1.30)

where
\widetilde{E}(t)
is defined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}(t):=\sum_{l=0}^{4}\widetilde{E}_{4+l}(t),\quad\widetilde{E}_{4+l}(t)=E_{4+l}(t)+\sum_{k=0}^{4+l}\left|\varepsilon^{2l}\partial_{t}^{k}\psi\right|^{2}_{4.5+l-k}.

(1.31)

Now we introduce the rigorous statement for the zero-surface-tension limit. For
\sigma\geq 0
, the motion of incompressible current-vortex sheets with surface tension are characterised by the equations of
(\xi^{\sigma},w^{\pm,\sigma},h^{\pm,\sigma})
with initial data
(\xi_{0}^{\sigma},w_{0}^{\pm,\sigma},h_{0}^{\pm,\sigma})
and a transport equation of
\mathfrak{S}^{\pm,\sigma}
:

\begin{cases}\mathfrak{R}^{\pm,\sigma}(\partial_{t}+w^{\pm,\sigma}\cdot\nabla^{\Xi^{\sigma}})w^{\pm,\sigma}-(h^{\pm,\sigma}\cdot\nabla^{\Xi^{\sigma}})h^{\pm,\sigma}+\nabla^{\Xi^{\sigma}}\Pi^{\pm,\sigma}=0&~~~\text{in}~[0,T]\times\Omega,\\
\nabla^{\Xi^{\sigma}}\cdot w^{\pm,\sigma}=0&~~~\text{in}~[0,T]\times\Omega,\\
(\partial_{t}+w^{\pm,\sigma}\cdot\nabla^{\Xi^{\sigma}})h^{\pm,\sigma}=(h^{\pm,\sigma}\cdot\nabla^{\Xi^{\sigma}})w^{\pm,\sigma}&~~~\text{in}~[0,T]\times\Omega,\\
\nabla^{\Xi^{\sigma}}\cdot h^{\pm,\sigma}=0&~~~\text{in}~[0,T]\times\Omega,\\
(\partial_{t}+w^{\pm,\sigma}\cdot\nabla^{\Xi^{\sigma}})\mathfrak{S}^{\pm,\sigma}=0&~~~\text{in}~[0,T]\times\Omega,\\
\left\llbracket\Pi^{\sigma}\right\rrbracket=\sigma\overline{\nabla}\cdot\left(\frac{\overline{\nabla}\xi^{\sigma}}{\sqrt{1+|\overline{\nabla}\xi^{\sigma}|^{2}}}\right)&~~~\text{on}~[0,T]\times\Sigma,\\
\partial_{t}\xi^{\sigma}=w^{\pm,\sigma}\cdot N^{\sigma}&~~~\text{on}~[0,T]\times\Sigma,\\
h^{\pm,\sigma}\cdot N^{\sigma}=0&~~~\text{on}~[0,T]\times\Sigma,\\
w_{3}^{\pm}=h_{3}^{\pm}=0&~~\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Sigma^{\pm},\\
(w^{\pm,\sigma},h^{\pm,\sigma},\mathfrak{S}^{\pm,\sigma},\xi^{\sigma})|_{t=0}=(w_{0}^{\pm,\sigma},h_{0}^{\pm,\sigma},\mathfrak{S}_{0}^{\pm,\sigma},\xi_{0}^{\sigma}),\end{cases}

(1.32)

where
\Xi^{\sigma}(t,x)=x_{3}+\chi(x_{3})\xi^{\sigma}(t,x^{\prime})
is the extension of
\xi^{\sigma}
in
\Omega
and
N^{\sigma}:=(-\overline{\partial}{}_{1}\xi^{\sigma},-\overline{\partial}{}_{2}\xi^{\sigma},1)^{\top}
. The quantity
\Pi^{\pm}:=\bar{\Pi}^{\pm}+\frac{1}{2}|h^{\pm}|^{2}
represent the total pressure functions for the incompressible equations with
\bar{\Pi}^{\pm}
the fluid pressure functions. The quantity
\mathfrak{R}^{\pm}
satisfies the evolution equation
(\partial_{t}+w^{\pm,\sigma}\cdot\nabla^{\Xi^{\sigma}})\mathfrak{R}^{\pm,\sigma}=0
with initial data
\mathfrak{R}_{0}^{\pm,\sigma}:=\rho^{\pm,\sigma}(0,\mathfrak{S}_{0}^{\pm,\sigma})
.

Denote
(\psi^{\varepsilon,\sigma},v^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}},b^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}},\rho^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}},S^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}})
to be the solution of (
1.15
) indexed by
\sigma
and
\varepsilon
. Under the stability conditions, we prove that
(\psi^{\varepsilon,\sigma},v^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}},b^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}},\rho^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}},S^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}})
converges to
(\xi^{0},w^{\pm,0},h^{\pm,0},\mathfrak{R}^{\pm,0},\mathfrak{S}^{\pm,0})
as
\varepsilon,\sigma\rightarrow 0
provided the convergence of initial data. Here
(\xi^{0},w^{\pm,0},h^{\pm,0},\mathfrak{R}^{\pm,0},\mathfrak{S}^{\pm,0})
represents the solution to incompressible current-vortex sheets system (
1.32
) with initial data
(\xi_{0}^{0},w_{0}^{\pm,0},h_{0}^{\pm,0},\mathfrak{S}_{0}^{\pm,0})
when
\sigma=0
.

Corollary 1.3

(
Incompressible and zero-surface-tension limits
)
.

Let
(\psi_{0}^{\varepsilon,\sigma},v_{0}^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}},b_{0}^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}},\rho_{0}^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}},S_{0}^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}})
be the initial data of (
1.15
) for each fixed
(\varepsilon,\sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
, satisfying

a.

The sequence of initial data
(\psi_{0}^{\varepsilon,\sigma},v_{0}^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}},b_{0}^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}},S_{0}^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}})\in H^{9.5}(\Sigma)\times H_{*}^{8}(\Omega^{\pm})\times H_{*}^{8}(\Omega^{\pm})\times H_{*}^{8}(\Omega^{\pm})
satisfies the hypothesis of Theorem
1.1
.

b.

(\psi_{0}^{\varepsilon,\sigma},v_{0}^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}},b_{0}^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}},S_{0}^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}})\to(\xi_{0}^{0},w_{0}^{\pm,0},h_{0}^{\pm,0},\mathfrak{S}_{0}^{\pm,0})
in
H^{5.5}(\Sigma)\times H^{4}(\Omega^{\pm})\times H^{4}(\Omega^{\pm})\times H^{4}(\Omega^{\pm})
as
\varepsilon,\sigma\to 0
.

c.

The incompressible initial data satisfies
|\left\llbracket\bar{w}_{0}^{0}\right\rrbracket|>0
on
\Sigma
, the constraints
\nabla^{\xi_{0}}\cdot h_{0}^{0,\pm}=0
in
\Omega^{\pm}
,
h^{0,\pm}\cdot N^{0}|_{\{t=0\}\times(\Sigma\cup\Sigma^{\pm})}=0
, the stability condition

\displaystyle 2\delta_{0}\leq\sqrt{\mathfrak{R}_{0}^{\pm,0}}\left|\bar{h}_{0}^{\mp,0}\times\left\llbracket\bar{w}_{0}^{0}\right\rrbracket\right|\leq(1-2\delta_{0})|\bar{h}_{0}^{+,0}\times\bar{h}_{0}^{-,0}|~~\text{ on }\Sigma,

(1.33)

where
\delta_{0}>0
is the same constant as in (
1.27
).

Then it holds that

\displaystyle(\psi^{\varepsilon,\sigma},v^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}},b^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}},S^{\pm,{\varepsilon,\sigma}})\to(\xi^{0},w^{\pm,0},h^{\pm,0},\mathfrak{S}^{\pm,0}),

(1.34)

weakly-* in
L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{4.5}(\Sigma)\times(H^{4}(\Omega^{\pm}))^{3})
and strongly in
C([0,T];H_{\text{loc}}^{4.5-\delta}(\Sigma)\times(H_{\text{loc}}^{4-\delta}(\Omega^{\pm}))^{3})
after possibly passing to a subsequence. Here
T>0
is the time obtained in Theorem
1.2
.

Remark 1.1

(Stability conditions in 2D)
.

When taking the zero-surface-tension limit, the stability condition for compressible current-vortex sheets in 2D is

\displaystyle\left(\frac{|b_{1}^{+}|}{a^{+}}+\frac{|b_{1}^{-}|}{a^{-}}\right)\geq(1+\delta_{0})|\left\llbracket v_{1}\right\rrbracket|>0~~\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Sigma,

(1.35)

which is again propagated by the initial constraint

\displaystyle\left(\frac{|b_{1}^{+}|}{a^{+}}+\frac{|b_{1}^{-}|}{a^{-}}\right)\bigg|_{t=0}\geq(1+2\delta_{0})|\left\llbracket v_{01}\right\rrbracket|>0~~\text{ on }\Sigma.

(1.36)

The corresponding stability condition for the incompressible data is

\displaystyle\left(\frac{|h_{01}^{+,0}|}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{R}_{0}^{+,0}}}+\frac{|h_{01}^{-,0}|}{\sqrt{\mathfrak{R}_{0}^{-,0}}}\right)\geq(1+2\delta_{0})\left|\left\llbracket w_{01}^{0}\right\rrbracket\right|>0.

(1.37)

1.4.4
Main result 2: Dropping boundedness assumptions of high-order time derivatives

The uniform-in-
\varepsilon
estimates obtained in Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
require
\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot v_{0}=O(\varepsilon^{2})
and
\partial_{t}^{k}v|_{t=0}=O(1)
for
k\leq 4
. Such assumption is much stronger than the widely used definition of “well-prepared” initial data (
\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot v_{0}=O(\varepsilon)
and
\partial_{t}v|_{t=0}=O(1)
). When
\left\llbracket\rho\right\rrbracket=O(\varepsilon)
on the interface
\Sigma
(see Remark
1.3
below), we can still prove the incompressible limit under the assumption
\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot v_{0}=O(\varepsilon),~\partial_{t}v|_{t=0}=O(1)
without any boundedness assumptions on high-order (
\geq 2
) time derivatives. However, the energy functional should also be modified. We define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}(t):=\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)+E_{5}(t)+E_{6}(t)+E_{7}(t)+E_{8}(t)

(1.38)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{E}}(t):=\widetilde{\mathfrak{E}}_{4}(t)+\widetilde{E}_{5}(t)+\widetilde{E}_{6}(t)+\widetilde{E}_{7}(t)+\widetilde{E}_{8}(t)

(1.39)

where
\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)
is defined as the following

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pm}\left\|(v^{\pm},b^{\pm},p^{\pm})\right\|_{4,\pm}^{2}+\left\|\partial_{t}(v^{\pm},b^{\pm},\varepsilon p^{\pm})\right\|_{3,\pm}^{2}+\sum_{k=2}^{4}\left\|\varepsilon\partial_{t}^{k}\left(v^{\pm},b^{\pm},(\mathcal{F}_{p}^{\pm})^{\frac{(k-3)_{+}}{2}}p^{\pm}\right)\right\|_{4-k,\pm}^{2}

(1.40)

\displaystyle+\left|\sqrt{\sigma}\psi\right|_{5}^{2}+\left|\sqrt{\sigma}\partial_{t}\psi\right|_{4}^{2}+\sum_{k=2}^{4}\left|\sqrt{\sigma}\varepsilon\partial_{t}^{k}\psi\right|_{5-k}^{2},

and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{E}}_{4}(t)=\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)+\left|\psi\right|_{4.5}^{2}+\left|\partial_{t}\psi\right|_{3.5}^{2}+\left|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\right|_{2.5}^{2}+\left|\varepsilon\partial_{t}^{3}\psi\right|_{1.5}^{2}+\left|\varepsilon\partial_{t}^{4}\psi\right|_{0.5}^{2}.

(1.41)

Theorem 1.4

(
Improved uniform estimates
)
.

Assume the fluids in
\Omega^{\pm}
are isentropic and the initial density functions satisfy
|\left\llbracket\rho_{0}\right\rrbracket|_{1.5}\leq C_{0}\varepsilon
on
\Sigma
for some
C_{0}>0
. Under the hypothesis of Theorem
1.1
, the assumption
\mathfrak{E}(0)\leq M^{\prime}
for some constant
M^{\prime}>0
, there exists
T_{\sigma}^{\prime}>0
depending only on
M^{\prime}
and
\sigma^{-1}
such that the solution
(v^{\pm}(t),b^{\pm}(t),\rho^{\pm}(t),\psi(t))
to system (
1.15
) verifies the uniform-in-
\varepsilon
energy estimate

\sup_{t\in[0,T_{\sigma}^{\prime}]}\mathfrak{E}(t)\leq C(\sigma^{-1})P(\mathfrak{E}(0)).

(1.42)

Furthermore, under the stability condition (
1.27
) and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{E}}(0)\leq M^{\prime}
, there exists
T^{\prime}>0
depending only on
M^{\prime}
such that such that the solution
(v^{\pm}(t),b^{\pm}(t),\rho^{\pm}(t),\psi(t))
to system (
1.15
) verifies the uniform-in-
({\varepsilon,\sigma})
energy estimate

\sup_{t\in[0,T^{\prime}]}\widetilde{\mathfrak{E}}(t)\leq P(\widetilde{\mathfrak{E}}(0)).

(1.43)

Remark 1.2
.

Since
\partial_{t}v|_{t=0}=O(1)
still remains bounded, the above uniform estimates directly give the same stronge convergence results as in Corollary
1.3
. We do not repeat the statement of convergence theorems here. The result is also true for 2D case under the stability condition (
1.35
).

Remark 1.3

(The smallness assumption on the density jump)
.

The assumption
|\left\llbracket\rho_{0}\right\rrbracket|_{1.5}\leq C_{0}\varepsilon
on
\Sigma
implies that
|\left\llbracket\rho(t)\right\rrbracket|_{1.5}\leq C_{1}\varepsilon
on
[0,T^{\prime}]\times\Sigma
for some
C_{1}>0
. To achieve this, one has to assume the fluids are isentropic and that is why the entropy
S
is deleted in
\mathfrak{E}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{E}}
. Indeed, taking the incompressible limit yields
\left\llbracket\mathfrak{R}\right\rrbracket=0
on
\Sigma
for the density functions
\mathfrak{R}^{\pm}
. If the fluids are non-isentropic, then
\mathfrak{R}^{\pm}
are not constants and only satisfy
(\partial_{t}+\bar{w}^{\pm}\cdot\overline{\nabla})\mathfrak{R}^{\pm}=0
on
\Sigma
. Since vortex-sheet problems require
\left\llbracket\bar{w}\right\rrbracket\neq 0
on
\Sigma
, it is not possible to have
\mathfrak{R}^{+}(t)=\mathfrak{R}^{-}(t)
on
\Sigma
even if it holds at
t=0
.

List of Notations:

In the rest of this paper, we sometimes write
\mathcal{T}^{k}
to represent a tangential derivative
\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}
in
\Omega^{\pm}
with order
\langle\alpha\rangle=k
when we do not need to specify what the derivative
\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}
contains. We also list all the notations used in this manuscript.

•

\Omega^{\pm}:=\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\times\{0<\pm x_{d}<H\}
,
\Sigma:=\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\times\{x_{d}=0\}
and
\Sigma^{\pm}:=\mathbb{T}^{d-1}\times\{x_{d}=\pm H\}
,
d=2,3
.

•

\|\cdot\|_{s,\pm}
: We denote
\|f\|_{s,\pm}:=\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{s}(\Omega^{\pm})}
for any function
f(t,x)\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Omega^{\pm}
.

•

|\cdot|_{s}
: We denote
|f|_{s}:=|f(t,\cdot)|_{H^{s}(\Sigma)}
for any function
f(t,x^{\prime})\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Sigma
.

•

\|\cdot\|_{m,*}
: For any function
f(t,x)\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Omega
,
\|f\|_{m,*,\pm}^{2}:=\sum\limits_{\langle\alpha\rangle\leq m}\|\partial_{*}^{\alpha}f(t,\cdot)\|_{0,\pm}^{2}
denotes the
m
-th order space-time anisotropic Sobolev norm of
f
.

•

P(\cdots)
: A generic polynomial with positive coefficients in its arguments;

•

[T,f]g:=T(fg)-fT(g)
, and
[T,f,g]:=T(fg)-T(f)g-fT(g)
, where
T
denotes a differential operator and
f,g
are arbitrary functions.

•

\overline{\partial}{}
:
\overline{\partial}{}=\partial_{1},\cdots,\partial_{d-1}
denotes the spatial tangential derivative.

•

A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L}}{{=}}B
:
A
is equal to
B
plus some lower-order terms that are easily controlled.

2
Strategy of the proof

By the compactness argument, it suffices to establish the uniform-in-
({\varepsilon,\sigma})
estimates under the stability condition (
1.27
). Let us briefly recall the analysis of
E(t)
, defined by (
1.25
), in the first paper of this sequence
[
63
]
. The first step is to reduce the normal derivatives via div-curl analysis. The divergence part is automatically converted to tangential estimates with suitable weights of Mach number thanks to the continuity equation. The control of curl part requires the anisotropic Sobolev norms because there is an anisotropic structure of Lorentz force, first discovered in the preparatoty work
[
55
]
, indicating that we shall trade one normal derivative (in the curl operator
\nabla\times
) for
\varepsilon^{2}
-weighted, second-order tangential derivative
\varepsilon^{2}D_{t}^{2}
. This fact exactly explains why we need 8-th order tangential regularity with
\varepsilon^{8}
weight (corresponding to the term
\widetilde{E}_{8}(t)
in the energy functional) to close the control of 4-th order full Sobolev norms (
\widetilde{E}_{4}(t)
in the energy).

In
[
63
]
, we adopted the div-curl inequality (
B.1
) and the reduction of normal derivatives is also recorded in Section
3
of this paper. It remains to establish the tangential estimates for
\varepsilon^{2l}\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}\mathcal{T}^{\beta}\partial_{t}^{k}
where
\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}=(\omega(x_{3})\partial_{3})^{\alpha_{4}}\partial_{t}^{\alpha_{0}}\partial_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}
and
\alpha,\beta,k,l
satisfy

\langle\alpha\rangle=2l,~\langle\beta\rangle=4-l-k,~0\leq k\leq 4-l,~0\leq l\leq 4~\text{ and }~\beta_{0}=0.

(2.1)

It should be noted that the
\varepsilon^{2l}\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}
-part appears due to the weighted anisotropic structure in the vorticity analysis for
E_{4+l}
and the
\mathcal{T}^{\beta}\partial_{t}^{k}
-part comes from the interior tangential derivatives in div-curl inequality (this part is parallel to Euler equations). See the analysis in
[
63
, Section 2.1-2.2]
for more details.

2.1
Zero-surface-tension limit under the stability conditions

Let us rewrite
\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}=\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}\mathcal{T}^{\beta}\partial_{t}^{k}
. Among the major terms arising in tangential estimates, the most difficult one is the following VS term contributed by the tangential discontinuity of velocity fields

\displaystyle\text{ VS}:=\varepsilon^{4l}\int_{\Sigma}\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}q^{-}(\left\llbracket\bar{v}\right\rrbracket\cdot\overline{\nabla})\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}\psi\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime},

(2.2)

where
\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}=\partial_{t}^{k}\overline{\partial}{}^{4+l-k}
,
0\leq k\leq 4+l,~0\leq l\leq 4
(Note that
\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}
vanishes on
\Sigma
when
\gamma_{4}>0
). This term must appear in current-vortex sheet problems due to
\left\llbracket\bar{v}\right\rrbracket\neq 0
, whereas it vanished in the study of MHD contact discontinuity
[
57
]
(
\left\llbracket v\right\rrbracket|_{\Sigma}=0,~B\cdot N|_{\Sigma}\neq 0
). In
[
63
]
, we use the ellipticity of the mean curvature to get enhanced regularity of
\psi
and
H^{\frac{1}{2}}
-
H^{-\frac{1}{2}}
duality to control this term when
\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}
contains at least one spatial derivative, and present rather delicate analysis to control the full-time derivatives with the help of some symmetric structures and lots of technical modifications. In this paper, we want to discuss the zero-surface-tension limit under the stability condition (
1.27
) and the dependece on
\sigma^{-1}
should be dropped. Thus, we must avoid using the ellipticity of the equation
\left\llbracket q\right\rrbracket=\sigma\mathcal{H}
and find a new way to control or modify or eliminate VS term with the help of (
1.27
). Roughly speaking, the stability condition (
1.27
) brings the following two benefits that Euler equations do not enjoy:

a.

Enhance the regularity of the free surface to
H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma)
in
\overline{\partial}{}^{s}
-estimates (possibly with suitable
\varepsilon
-weights). This gives 1/2-order higher regularity of
\psi
than the Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition does.

b.

Completely eliminate the problematic term VS
.

The enhanced regularity in (a) is easy to prove, as the “non-collinearity” allows us to resolve
\overline{\nabla}\psi
in terms of
b^{\pm}
without any derivative. The benefit (b) is rather important. When taking the vanishing surface tension limit, the enhanced regularity obtained in (a) is still not enough to control VS. Instead, we completely eliminate the contribution of
\left\llbracket\bar{v}\right\rrbracket
in the term VS by inserting a suitable term involving the magnetic fields. Specifically, we want to insert a term
\mu^{\pm}\bar{b}^{\pm}
into VS to get

\text{ VS}^{\prime}:=\int_{\Sigma}\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}q^{-}\left(\left\llbracket\bar{v}-\mu\bar{b}\right\rrbracket\cdot\overline{\nabla}\right)\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}\psi\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

(2.3)

and find suitable functions
\mu^{\pm}
such that
\left\llbracket\bar{v}-\mu\bar{b}\right\rrbracket=\mathbf{0}
on
\Sigma
. Under the stability condition (
1.27
), the functions
\mu^{\pm}
uniquely exist. To construct the term
\text{ VS}^{\prime}
from MHD equations (
1.15
), we shall replace the variable
v^{\pm}
in the momentum equation by
v^{\pm}-\mu^{\pm}b^{\pm}
. However, this operation makes the MHD system not symmetric and consequently the energy estimates cannot be closed. To overcome this difficulty, we introduce the “Friedrichs secondary symmetrization”
[
20
]
, which was first applied to compressible ideal MHD by Trakhinin
[
52
]
, to re-symmetrize the MHD system.

The final step is to determine the range for
\mu^{\pm}
such that the energy estimates for the secondary-symmetrized MHD system can be closed. Using the idea of Alinhac good unknowns
[
5
]
, the energy for
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{P}
(defined in Section
4.2.2
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}\rho^{\pm}|\mathbf{V}^{\pm}|^{2}+|\mathbf{B}^{\pm}|^{2}+\mathcal{F}_{p}^{\pm}|\mathbf{P}^{\pm}|^{2}-2\mu^{\pm}\rho^{\pm}\mathbf{V}^{\pm}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{\pm}-2\mu^{\pm}\rho^{\pm}\mathcal{F}_{p}^{\pm}\mathbf{P}^{\pm}(b^{\pm}\cdot\mathbf{V}^{\pm})\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{V}_{t},

(2.4)

where
\mathcal{F}_{p}^{\pm}=1/(\rho^{\pm}(c_{s}^{\pm})^{2})
and
\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{V}_{t}:=\partial_{3}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}x
. We must guarantee the above quadratic form of
(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{P})
to be positive-definite, which is equivalent to guarantee the hyperbolicity. This requires
\mu^{\pm}
to satisfy
(\mu^{\pm})^{2}\rho^{\pm}(1+(c_{A}^{\pm}/c_{s}^{\pm})^{2})<1
. This inequality gives the range of
\mu^{\pm}
, which exactly coincides with the stability condition (
1.27
).

Remark 2.1

(Stabilization effects on 2D subsonic vortex sheets)
.

In the 2D case, the non-collinearity property no longer holds because the interface is 1D. The functions
\mu^{\pm}
still exist but are not unique. For a rectilinear piecewise-smooth background solution
(\pm\underline{v},0,\pm\underline{b},0,\underline{p}^{\pm},\underline{S}^{\pm})^{\top}
, condition (
1.35
) implies that
|\underline{v}|^{2}<c_{s}^{2}\frac{c_{A}^{2}}{c_{A}^{2}+c_{s}^{2}}<c_{s}^{2}
, that is, the background solution must be a subsonic flow, whereas the linear stability only holds for supersonic flow, that is,
|v|/c_{s}>\sqrt{2}
, for 2D vortex sheets of compressible Euler equations. Thus, sufficiently strong magnetic fields have stabilization effects on 2D subsonic vortex sheets.

2.2
A paradifferential approach for the low Mach number limit of vortex sheets

2.2.1
Difficulty caused by free-surface motion

As stated in Section
1.4.4
, when
\varepsilon
is suitably small, the incompressible limit can be established under the assumption
\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot v_{0}=O(\varepsilon),~\partial_{t}v|_{t=0}=O(1)
without any redundant restrictions on higher-order time derivatives. This is not difficult under the fixed-domain setting, but for free-boundary problems, we need to use the energy defined in (
1.38
)-(
1.41
) and new essential difficulties caused by the free-interface motion. For example, in the
\overline{\partial}{}^{3}\partial_{t}
-estimate (without
\varepsilon
-weight) that arises in
\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)
, we have to control integrals in the following form

\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}(\overline{\partial}{}^{2}\partial\partial_{t}v_{i}^{\pm})(\overline{\partial}{}\mathbf{N}_{i})(\overline{\partial}{}^{2}\partial\partial_{t}q^{\pm})\,\mathrm{d}x,\text{ which arises from }\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}(\overline{\partial}{}^{3}\partial_{t}v_{i}^{\pm})[\overline{\partial}{}^{3},\mathbf{N}_{i},\overline{\partial}{}^{3}\partial_{t}q]\,\mathrm{d}x.

The simultaneous appearance of
\overline{\partial}{}^{2}\partial\partial_{t}v
and
\overline{\partial}{}^{2}\partial\partial_{t}q
causes a loss of
\varepsilon
-weight.
The appearance of such loss of
\varepsilon
-weight is actually necessary when
\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}v
(
\gamma_{0}<\langle\gamma\rangle
) is assigned a different
\varepsilon
-weight from that of
\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}q
, because the normal vector
\mathbf{N}
may not necessarily absorb a time derivative when
\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}
contains both
\overline{\partial}{}
and
\partial_{t}
.
This difficulty is completely caused by the free-interface motion
and there is no such difficulty for the fixed-domain problem
[
55
]
. It should be noted that for one-phase flow without surface tension, where the pressure function vanished on the free surface, this difficulty can be avoided by combining the elliptic estimates and the interior estimates of the wave equation for the pressure. This was proven in the author’s previous work
[
62
]
, but the proof heavily relies on
q|_{\Sigma}=0
.

2.2.2
Improved estimates for double limits: paralinearization of the free-interface motion

To control
\mathfrak{E}_{4}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{E}}_{4}
in (
1.40
)-(
1.41
), we only need to re-consider the estimates of
\|v_{t}\|_{3}
and
\|b_{t}\|_{3}
because
\partial_{t}^{k}v
and
\partial_{t}^{k}q
share the same weights of Mach number for
k\geq 2
and the control of
\geq 2
time derivatives should be the same as
E_{4}(t)
in
[
63
]
. To avoid interior tangential estimates, we apply the div-curl inequality (
B.2
) to
v_{t},b_{t}
and reduce the control to their normal traces
|v_{t}\cdot N|_{2.5}
and
|b_{t}\cdot N|_{2.5}
. In view of the boundary conditions, we must seek for other ways to control
|\partial_{t}^{k}\psi|_{4.5-k}
for
0\leq k\leq 2
and they must be
\sigma
-indepedent when taking the double limits
{\varepsilon,\sigma}\to 0
.

We now consider the time-differentiated kinematic boundary condition, which together with the momentum equation gives

\rho^{\pm}\psi_{tt}=-N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}q^{\pm}+(\bar{b}_{i}^{\pm}\bar{b}_{j}^{\pm}-\rho^{\pm}\bar{v}_{i}^{\pm}\bar{v}_{j}^{\pm})\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\psi+\cdots

Motivated by Shatah-Zeng
[
46
]
, we try to separate the boundary values of
q^{\pm}
from the interior contribution of
q^{\pm}
. Specifically,
q^{\pm}
satisfies a two-phase wave equation (we write
\mathcal{F}_{p}^{\pm}=\varepsilon^{2}
for convenience)

\varepsilon^{2}(D_{t}^{\varphi\pm})^{2}q^{\pm}-\Delta^{\varphi}q^{\pm}=\varepsilon^{2}(D_{t}^{\varphi\pm})^{2}(\frac{1}{2}|b^{\pm}|^{2})+(\partial^{\varphi}_{i}v_{j}^{\pm})(\partial^{\varphi}_{j}v_{i}^{\pm})-(\partial^{\varphi}_{i}b_{j}^{\pm})(\partial^{\varphi}_{j}b_{i}^{\pm})\text{ in }\Omega^{\pm},\quad\partial_{3}q^{\pm}|_{\Sigma^{\pm}}=0,~~\left\llbracket q\right\rrbracket|_{\Sigma}=\sigma\mathcal{H}(\psi).

and we introduce the decomposition
q^{\pm}=q_{\psi}^{\pm}+q_{w}^{\pm}
with

\displaystyle-\Delta^{\varphi}q_{\psi}^{\pm}=0\text{ in }\Omega^{\pm},~~q_{\psi}^{\pm}=q^{\pm}\text{ on }\Sigma,~~\partial_{3}q_{\psi}^{\pm}=0\text{ on }\Sigma^{\pm},

\displaystyle-\Delta^{\varphi}q_{w}^{\pm}=-\varepsilon^{2}(D_{t}^{\varphi\pm})^{2}(q^{\pm}-\frac{1}{2}|b^{\pm}|^{2})+(\partial^{\varphi}_{i}v_{j}^{\pm})(\partial^{\varphi}_{j}v_{i}^{\pm})-(\partial^{\varphi}_{i}b_{j}^{\pm})(\partial^{\varphi}_{j}b_{i}^{\pm})\text{ in }\Omega^{\pm},~~q_{w}^{\pm}=0\text{ on }\Sigma,~~\partial_{3}q_{w}^{\pm}=0\text{ on }\Sigma^{\pm}.

Under this setting, we can write
-N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}q^{\pm}=\pm\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\pm}(q^{\pm}|_{\Sigma})-N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}q_{w}^{\pm}
where
\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\pm}
represents the Dirichlet-to-Neumann (DtN) operators with respect to
\Omega^{\pm}
and
\psi
(defined in Section
5.2
). The traces of
q^{\pm}
on
\Sigma
can be resolved by inverting the DtN operators, which then gives us the following evolution equation

\displaystyle(\rho^{+}+\rho^{-})\partial_{t}^{2}\psi=

\displaystyle~\frac{\sigma}{2}\left(\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{+}+\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{-}\right)(\mathcal{H}(\psi))+\left(\bar{b}_{i}^{+}\bar{b}_{j}^{+}-\rho^{+}\bar{v}_{i}^{+}\bar{v}_{j}^{+}+\bar{b}_{i}^{-}\bar{b}_{j}^{-}-\rho^{-}\bar{v}_{i}^{-}\bar{v}_{j}^{-}\right)\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\psi

\displaystyle-N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}q_{w}^{+}-N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}q_{w}^{-}+\underline{(\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{+}-\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{-})(\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{+}+\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{-})^{-1}(\left\llbracket\rho\right\rrbracket\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)}+\cdots

(2.5)

Using the paralinearization tools in Alazard-Burq-Zuily
[
2
,
3
]
and Alazard-Métivier
[
4
]
, the principal symbol of the major term
\left(\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{+}+\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{-}\right)(\mathcal{H}(\psi))
is negative and of the third order. Besides, the stability condition (
1.27
) ensures the ellipticity of the second term on the right side. The contribution of
q_{w}
is completely reduced to the source term of the wave equation thanks to
q_{w}|_{\Sigma}=0
. Thus, we can simultaneously obtain the estimates of
|\psi|_{4.5},~|\sqrt{\sigma}\psi|_{5}
and
|\psi_{t}|_{3.5}
by taking a suitable 3.5-th order paradifferential operator in (
2.5
) and we refer to Section
5.3
for details.

For the control of
|\psi_{tt}|_{2.5}
, it suffices to take
\partial_{t}
in (
2.5
) and take a suitable 2.5-th order paradifferential operator. However, the underlined term in (
2.5
), after taking
\partial_{t}
, contains third-order time derivative, whose
H^{2.5}(\Sigma)
norm is bounded by
|\left\llbracket\rho\right\rrbracket|_{1.5}|\partial_{t}^{3}\psi|_{1.5}
. In general, we have a loss of
\varepsilon
-weight, as the energy
\mathfrak{E}(t)
only gives
\partial_{t}^{3}\psi=O(\varepsilon^{-1})
. So, if we additionally require
|\left\llbracket\rho\right\rrbracket|_{1.5}=O(\varepsilon)
, which can be fulfilled for isentropic flows according to Remark
1.3
, this extra
\varepsilon
-weight could compensate the loss of
\varepsilon
-weight arising in this term. Hence, under the extra assumption
|\left\llbracket\rho\right\rrbracket|_{1.5}=O(\varepsilon)
, we can control the accelaration of the free interface uniformly in
\varepsilon
without assuming the boundedness of high-order time derivatives of
v
.

Remark 2.2
.

As we can see,
the framework above is also applicable to compressible vortex sheets with surface tension for Euler equations, not just MHD system itself
. In fact, when
b^{\pm}=0
, the second term on the right side of (
2.5
) is controlled by the energy contributed by surface tension. Since the vorticity analysis of Euler equations does not produce high-order terms. Then the estimates of
\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)+E_{5}(t)
(or
\widetilde{\mathfrak{E}}_{4}(t)+\widetilde{E}_{5}(t)
for the double limits) can be closed by applying the above framework. Do note that
E_{5}(t)
is necessary to close the uniform estimates because of the term
\varepsilon^{2}(D_{t}^{\varphi})^{2}q
. This part vanishes as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Remark 2.3

(Comparison with the Syrovatskiĭ condition for incompressible MHD)
.

The original Syrovatskiĭ stability condition (cf. Syrovatskiĭ
[
50
]
or Landau-Lifshitz-Pitaevskiĭ
[
30
, §71]
) for
\rho^{\pm}=1
is
|\bar{h}^{+}\times\left\llbracket\bar{w}\right\rrbracket|^{2}+|\bar{h}^{-}\times\left\llbracket\bar{w}\right\rrbracket|^{2}<2|\bar{h}^{+}\times\bar{h}^{-}|^{2}
on
\Sigma
, which is less restrictive than the formal incompressible limit of (
1.27
) as
\rho^{\pm}\to 1
. We recall that, in the analysis of equation (
2.5
), the compressibility introduces an extra term
\varepsilon^{2}(D_{t}^{\varphi})^{2}p
in the source term of
q_{w}
that should be controlled via interior estimates. Since
\Omega^{+},\Omega^{-}
are disconnected domains, it is reasonable to have restrictions for solutions in
\Omega^{+}
and
\Omega^{-}
respectively. Besides, the extra term
\varepsilon^{2}(D_{t}^{\varphi})^{2}p
presents a loss of derivative in standard Sobolev spaces and again indicates that one should trade a normal derivative for two tangential derivatives with
\varepsilon^{2}
weights.

The difference between the two stability conditions is related to the singular nature of incompressible limit. One can further see such difference from the derivation of these stability conditions and we refer to Trakhinin
[
52
]
for details. Similar situation also occurs in the case of 2D.

2.2.3
Comparison with one-phase problems

Finally, we briefly discuss the differences between one-phase problems and vortex-sheet problems. Without loss of generality, we assume everything in
\Omega^{-}
is vanishing for one-phase problems and thus the term VS is vanishing and other major boundary terms can be similarly analyzed under the Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition
\partial_{3}q^{+}\geq c_{0}>0
. When considering the incompressible limit without boundedness assumptions on high-order time derivatives, compressible vortex-sheet problems exhibit new essential difficulty because the Dirichlet-type boundary condition
q|_{\Sigma}=\sigma\mathcal{H}
becomes a jump condition
\left\llbracket q\right\rrbracket=\sigma\mathcal{H}
. Thus, we have to invert the DtN operators to resolve the traces of
q^{\pm}
on
\Sigma
. Since the fluids are compressible, we cannot directly apply
(\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\pm})^{-1}
to
(1/\rho^{\pm})\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\pm}(q^{\pm}|_{\Sigma})
as in the incompressible case
[
34
,
31
]
because the product of a harmonic function and
1/\rho^{\pm}
is no longer harmonic. Then the bad term
(\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{+}-\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{-})(\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{+}+\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{-})^{-1}(\left\llbracket\rho\right\rrbracket\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)
must appear on the right side of (
2.5
) which already contains a second-order time derivative. Without the assumption
\left\llbracket\rho\right\rrbracket=O(\varepsilon)
on
\Sigma
, which can be achieve by requiring the fluids to be isentropic and the density functions converge to the same constant, there exhibits a loss of
\varepsilon
-weight in the control of
\psi_{tt}
in general. That is to say, under the assumption
\nabla^{\varphi_{0}}\cdot v_{0}=O(\varepsilon)
, the accelaration of the free interface may be still not uniformly bounded in
\varepsilon
for compressible vortex sheets. Such loss of
\varepsilon
-weight never appears in the fixed-domain problems
[
1
,
55
]
, one-phase problems
[
62
,
36
]
or incompressible (current-)vortex sheets
[
48
,
34
,
31
]
.

3
Reduction of normal derivatives

Compared with the analysis in
[
63
]
, the control of
\widetilde{E}(t)
only needs modifications in tangential estimates, as the VS term must be controlled uniformly in
\sigma
. In this section, we record the reduction of normal derivatives that has been analyzed in
[
63
]
without repeating the proof.

Div-Curl analysis and reduction of pressure

Using the div-curl decomposition (Lemma
B.1
), we have for
0\leq l\leq 3,~0\leq k\leq 3-l,~\langle\alpha\rangle=2l,~\alpha_{3}=0
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\varepsilon^{2l}\partial_{t}^{k}\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}(v^{\pm},b^{\pm})\right\|_{4-k-l,\pm}^{2}\leq C\bigg(

\displaystyle\left\|\varepsilon^{2l}\partial_{t}^{k}\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}(v^{\pm},b^{\pm})\right\|_{0,\pm}^{2}+\left\|\varepsilon^{2l}\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot\partial_{t}^{k}\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}(v^{\pm},b^{\pm})\right\|_{3-k-l,\pm}^{2}

\displaystyle+\left\|\varepsilon^{2l}\nabla^{\varphi}\times\partial_{t}^{k}\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}(v^{\pm},b^{\pm})\right\|_{3-k-l,\pm}^{2}+\left\|\varepsilon^{2l}\overline{\partial}{}^{4-k-l}\partial_{t}^{k}\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}(v^{\pm},b^{\pm})\right\|_{0,\pm}^{2}\bigg)

(3.1)

with

C=C\left(\sum_{j=0}^{l}\sum_{k=0}^{3+j}|\varepsilon^{2j}\partial_{t}^{j}\psi|_{4+l-j}^{2},|\overline{\nabla}\psi|_{W^{1,\infty}}\right)>0

a positive continuious function in its arguments.
The conclusion for the div-curl analysis is

Proposition 3.1

(
[
63
, Proposition 3.8]
)
.

Fix
l\in\{0,1,2,3\}
. For any
0\leq k\leq l-1
, any multi-index
\alpha
satisfying
\langle\alpha\rangle=2l
and any constant
\delta\in(0,1)
, we can prove the following
uniform-in-
({\varepsilon,\sigma})
estimates for the curl part

\displaystyle\left\|\varepsilon^{2l}\nabla^{\varphi}\times\partial_{t}^{k}\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}v^{\pm}\right\|_{3-k-l,\pm}^{2}+\left\|\varepsilon^{2l}\nabla^{\varphi}\times\partial_{t}^{k}\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}b^{\pm}\right\|_{3-k-l,\pm}^{2}

(3.2)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\delta E_{4+l}(t)+P\left(\sum_{j=0}^{l}\widetilde{E}_{4+j}(0)\right)+P(E_{4}(t))\int_{0}^{t}P\left(\sum_{j=0}^{l}\widetilde{E}_{4+j}(\tau)\right)+E_{4+l+1}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau,

and for the divergence part

\displaystyle\left\|\varepsilon^{2l}\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot\partial_{t}^{k}\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}v^{\pm}\right\|_{3-k-l,\pm}^{2}+\left\|\varepsilon^{2l}\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot\partial_{t}^{k}\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}v^{\pm}\right\|_{3-k-l,\pm}^{2}

(3.3)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\delta E_{4+l}(t)+P\left(\sum_{j=0}^{l}\widetilde{E}_{4+j}(0)\right)+P(E_{4}(t))\int_{0}^{t}P\left(\sum_{j=0}^{l}\widetilde{E}_{4+j}(\tau)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

Remark 3.1
.

The above estimates are not uniform in
\sigma
in
[
63
, Proposition 3.8]
due to the lacking of stability condition. With the help of stability condition (
1.27
), we can obtain the non-
\sigma
-weighted boundary regularity, namely the quantities in the constant
C
in (
3.1
). This will be proved in Section
4.1
.

Reduction of pressure and divergence

Let us start with
l=0
. The spatial derivative of
q
is controlled by invoking the momentum equation:

\displaystyle-\partial_{3}q=

\displaystyle~(\partial_{3}\varphi)\left(\rho D_{t}^{\varphi}v_{3}-(b\cdot\nabla^{\varphi})b_{3}\right);

(3.4)

\displaystyle-\overline{\partial}{}_{i}q=

\displaystyle-(\partial_{3}\varphi)^{-1}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\varphi\,\partial_{3}q+\rho D_{t}^{\varphi}v_{i}-(b\cdot\nabla^{\varphi})b_{i},~~i=1,2.

(3.5)

Let
\mathcal{T}
be
\partial_{t}
or
\overline{\partial}{}
or
\omega(x_{3})\partial_{3}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}^{k}\partial_{3}q\|_{3-k}\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|\partial_{t}^{k}(\rho\mathcal{T}v_{3})\|_{3-k}+\|\partial_{t}^{k}(b\mathcal{T}b_{3})\|_{3-k}

(3.6)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}^{k}\overline{\partial}{}_{i}q\|_{3-k}\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|\partial_{t}^{k}(\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\varphi\partial_{3}q)\|_{3-k}+\|\partial_{t}^{k}(\rho\mathcal{T}v_{i})\|_{3-k}+\|\partial_{t}^{k}(b\mathcal{T}b_{i})\|_{3-k},

(3.7)

in which the leading order terms are
\|\partial_{t}^{k}\mathcal{T}(v,b)\|_{3-k}
and
|\partial_{t}^{k}\psi|_{4-k}
. This shows that we can convert the control of spatial derivative of
q
to
tangential estimates
of
v
and
b
.

Control of the entropy

The control of entropy is easy thanks to
D_{t}^{\varphi\pm}S^{\pm}=0
. We now record the result as below.

Proposition 3.2
.

For
0\leq l\leq 4,0\leq k\leq 4-l
, multi-index
\gamma
satisfying
\langle\gamma\rangle=4+l
and any
\delta\in(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\varepsilon^{2l}\partial_{t}^{k}\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}S^{\pm}\right\|_{4-k-l,\pm}^{2}\lesssim\delta\widetilde{E}_{4+l}(t)+P\left(\sum_{j=0}^{l}\widetilde{E}_{4+j}(0)\right)+\widetilde{E}_{4}(t)\int_{0}^{t}P\left(\sum_{j=0}^{l}\widetilde{E}_{4+j}(\tau)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\tau,

(3.8)

4
Tangential estimates and the zero-surface-tension limit

In this section, we aim to prove the following uniform-in-
({\varepsilon,\sigma})
tangential estimates

Proposition 4.1

(Tangential estimates)
.

For fixed
l\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}
and any
\delta\in(0,1)
, the following uniform-in-
({\varepsilon,\sigma})
energy inequalities hold:

\displaystyle\sum_{\pm}\sum_{\langle\alpha\rangle=2l}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k\leq 4-l\\
k+\alpha_{0}<4+l\end{subarray}}\left\|\left(\varepsilon^{2l}\overline{\partial}{}^{4-k-l}\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{k}(v^{\pm},b^{\pm},p^{\pm})\right)\right\|^{2}_{0,\pm}+\sum_{k=0}^{3+l}\left|\sqrt{\sigma}\varepsilon^{2l}\partial_{t}^{k}\psi\right|^{2}_{5-k-l}\,\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\delta\widetilde{E}_{4+l}(t)+P\left(\sum_{j=0}^{l}\widetilde{E}_{4+j}(0)\right)+P\left(\sum_{j=0}^{l}\widetilde{E}_{4+j}(t)\right)\int_{0}^{t}P\left(\sum_{j=0}^{l}\widetilde{E}_{4+j}(\tau)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\tau

(4.1)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{\pm}\sum_{k=0}^{4-l}\left\|\left(\varepsilon^{2l}\partial_{t}^{4+l}(v^{\pm},b^{\pm},(\mathcal{F}_{p})^{\frac{1}{2}}p^{\pm})\right)\right\|^{2}_{4-k-l,\pm}+\left|\sqrt{\sigma}\varepsilon^{2l}\partial_{t}^{4+l}\psi\right|^{2}_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\delta\widetilde{E}_{4+l}(t)+P\left(\sum_{j=0}^{l}\widetilde{E}_{4+j}(0)\right)+P\left(\sum_{j=0}^{l}\widetilde{E}_{4+j}(t)\right)\int_{0}^{t}P\left(\sum_{j=0}^{l}\widetilde{E}_{4+j}(\tau)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

(4.2)

Here the first inequality represents the case when there are at least one spatial tangential derivatives and the second inequality represents the case of full time derivatives.

An analogue of Proposition
4.1
has been proven in
[
63
, Proposition 3.3]
without assuming the stability condition (
1.27
), and thus the energy bounds in
[
63
, Proposition 3.3]
rely on
\sigma^{-1}
. Now, we explain how to use the stability condition (
1.27
) to get rid of the dependence on
1/\sigma
in the estimates recorded in Section
3
. Let us recall what quantities in tangential estimates of
E(t)
depend on
1/\sigma
in
[
63
]
without assuming the stability condition (
1.27
). Following the analysis in
[
63
, Section 2.2, 3.3-3.5]
, we can obtain the energy inequality for a given multi-index
\alpha
satisfying
\langle\alpha\rangle=2l
and for
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
0\leq k\leq 4-l,~0\leq l\leq 4
.

\displaystyle\sum_{\pm}\left\|\left(\varepsilon^{2l}\overline{\partial}{}^{4-k-l}\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{k}(v^{\pm},b^{\pm},(\mathcal{F}_{p})^{\frac{1}{2}}p^{\pm})\right)\right\|^{2}_{4-k-l,\pm}\leq

\displaystyle~\delta\widetilde{E}(t)+P(\widetilde{E}(0))+P(\widetilde{E}(t))\int_{0}^{t}P(\widetilde{E}(\tau))\,\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\text{ ST}+\text{ VS}+\text{ RT}+\sum_{\pm}(ZB^{\pm}+Z^{\pm})\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

(4.3)

Here these major terms are defined by

\displaystyle\text{ ST}:=

\displaystyle~\varepsilon^{4l}\int_{\Sigma}\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}(\sigma\mathcal{H})\partial_{t}\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}\psi\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime},~~\text{ RT}:=-\varepsilon^{4l}\int_{\Sigma}\left\llbracket\partial_{3}q\right\rrbracket\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}\psi\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}\partial_{t}\psi\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime},

(4.4)

\displaystyle\text{ VS}:=

\displaystyle~\varepsilon^{4l}\int_{\Sigma}\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}q^{-}(\left\llbracket\bar{v}\right\rrbracket\cdot\overline{\nabla})\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}\psi\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime},

(4.5)

\displaystyle ZB^{\pm}:=

\displaystyle~\mp\varepsilon^{4l}\int_{\Sigma}\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}q^{\pm}[\mathcal{T}^{\gamma},N_{i},v_{i}^{\pm}]\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime},\quad Z^{\pm}:=-\varepsilon^{4l}\int_{\Omega^{\pm}}\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}q^{\pm}[\mathcal{T}^{\gamma},\mathbf{N}_{i},\partial_{3}v_{i}^{\pm}]\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{V}_{t}.

(4.6)

where
\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}=\partial_{t}^{k}\overline{\partial}{}^{4+l-k}
,
0\leq k\leq 4+l,~0\leq l\leq 4
and
\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{V}_{t}:=\partial_{3}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}x
. Do note that when
\alpha_{4}>0
, all boundary terms vanish and
Z^{\pm}
can be directly controlled, so we only need to analyze the case when
\alpha
has the same form as
\gamma
. To get rid of the dependence on
1/\sigma
, the above quantities should be controlled in the following way:

•

The control of quantity ST does not depend on
1/\sigma
;

•

Quantity RT can be controlled if we have the estimates of
|\varepsilon^{2l}\partial_{t}^{k}\psi|_{4.5+l-k}
(under time integral) for
0\leq k\leq 4-l,~0\leq l\leq 4
;

•

Quantity
ZB^{\pm}+Z^{\pm}
can be controlled if we have the estimates of
|\varepsilon^{2l}\partial_{t}^{k}\psi|_{4+l-k}
(NOT under time integral) for
0\leq k\leq 4-l,~0\leq l\leq 4
;

•

Quantity VS must be completely eliminated.

Besides, the control of the commutators
\|\varepsilon^{2l}[\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot,\partial_{t}^{k}\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}](v^{\pm},b^{\pm})\|_{3-k-l,\pm}
and
\|\varepsilon^{2l}[\nabla^{\varphi}\times,\partial_{t}^{k}\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}](v^{\pm},b^{\pm})\|_{3-k-l,\pm}
in the proof of Proposition
3.1
(Note that this commutator appears without time integral!) also needs the estimates of
|\varepsilon^{2l}\partial_{t}^{k}\psi|_{4+l-k}
.

4.1
Enhanced regularity of the interface: non-collinearity of magnetic fields

It is easy to see that merely invoking the kinematic boundary condition and using trace lemma does not solve any issues mentioned above. In order to seek for
\sigma
-independent estimates for
\psi
and its time derivatives, we require the stability condition (
1.27
) when the space dimension is 3, that is, for some
\delta_{0}\in(0,\frac{1}{8})
,

\displaystyle 0<\delta_{0}\leq a^{\pm}\left|\bar{b}^{\mp}\times\left\llbracket\bar{v}\right\rrbracket\right|\leq(1-\delta_{0})\left|\bar{b}^{+}\times\bar{b}^{-}\right|~~\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Sigma,

(4.7)

where we view
\bar{b}^{\pm}=(b_{1}^{\pm},b_{2}^{\pm},0)^{\top},\left\llbracket\bar{v}\right\rrbracket=(\left\llbracket v_{1}\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket v_{2}\right\rrbracket,0)^{\top}
as vectors lying on the plane
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\{x_{3}=0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
to define the exterior product. The quantity
a^{\pm}
is defined by

\displaystyle a^{\pm}:=\sqrt{\rho^{\pm}\left(1+\left(\frac{c_{A}^{\pm}}{c_{s}^{\pm}}\right)^{2}\right)}

and
c_{A}^{\pm}:=|b^{\pm}|/\sqrt{\rho^{\pm}}
represents the Alfvén speed,
c_{s}^{\pm}:=\sqrt{\partial p^{\pm}/\partial\rho^{\pm}}
represents the sound speed. This condition implies the following two important features:

1.

Magnetics fields are not collinear on
\Sigma
, which allows us to gain 1/2-order regularity of the free interface.

2.

Quantitative relation between
b^{\pm}
and
\left\llbracket\bar{v}\right\rrbracket
on
\Sigma
allows us to completely eliminate the problematic term VS.

We aim to prove uniform-in-
(\varepsilon,\sigma)
estimates for the energy functional
\widetilde{E}(t)
for the compressible current-vortex sheet system (
1.15
). Recall
\widetilde{E}(t)
is defined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}(t):=\sum_{l=0}^{4}\widetilde{E}_{4+l}(t),~~\widetilde{E}_{4+l}(t):=E_{4+l}(t)+\sum_{k=0}^{4+l}\left|\varepsilon^{2l}\partial_{t}^{k}\psi\right|_{4.5+l-k}^{2},

(4.8)

where the term added to
E_{4+l}(t)
exactly gives the enhanced regularity of the free interface contributed by the non-collinearity stability condition (
4.7
).

Recall that the magnetic fields satisfy the constraint
b^{\pm}\cdot N=0
on
\Sigma
, that is,
b_{3}^{\pm}=b_{1}^{\pm}\overline{\partial}{}_{1}\psi+b_{2}^{\pm}\overline{\partial}{}_{2}\psi
. So, we can solve
\overline{\partial}{}\psi
in terms of
b^{\pm}
without any derivatives thanks to
\bar{b}^{+}\times\bar{b}^{-}\neq\mathbf{0}
. However, due to the anisotropy of the function spaces, we have to take derivatives on the constraint before we use trace lemma. We have the following estimates for the interface function
\psi
.

Lemma 4.2
.

For
s\geq 3
, one has

\displaystyle|\overline{\partial}{}\psi|_{s-\frac{1}{2}}^{2}\leq P(\|b^{\pm}\|_{s-1,*,\pm},\|b^{\pm}\|_{3,\pm},|\overline{\partial}{}\psi|_{W^{1,\infty}})\left(\|\partial_{3}\langle\overline{\partial}{}\rangle^{s-2}b^{\pm}\|_{0,\pm}\|\langle\overline{\partial}{}\rangle^{s}b^{\pm}\|_{0,\pm}+|\overline{\partial}{}\psi|_{s-1}^{2}+\|\langle\overline{\partial}{}\rangle^{s}b^{\pm}\|_{0,\pm}^{2}\right).

(4.9)

Here
\langle\overline{\partial}{}\rangle:=\sqrt{1-\overline{\Delta}{}}
, that is,
\widehat{\langle\overline{\partial}{}\rangle f}(\xi)=\sqrt{1+|\xi|^{2}}\hat{f}(\xi)
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
.

Proof.

Taking
\overline{\partial}{}^{s-\frac{1}{2}}
in the constraint
b^{\pm}\cdot N=0
for
s\geq 3
, we get

\displaystyle\begin{cases}b_{1}^{+}\overline{\partial}{}_{1}\langle\overline{\partial}{}\rangle^{s-\frac{1}{2}}\psi+b_{2}^{+}\overline{\partial}{}_{2}\langle\overline{\partial}{}\rangle^{s-\frac{1}{2}}\psi=f_{b}^{+}\\
b_{1}^{-}\overline{\partial}{}_{1}\langle\overline{\partial}{}\rangle^{s-\frac{1}{2}}\psi+b_{2}^{-}\overline{\partial}{}_{2}\langle\overline{\partial}{}\rangle^{s-\frac{1}{2}}\psi=f_{b}^{-}\end{cases}\xRightarrow{~b^{+}\nparallel b^{-}}\begin{cases}\overline{\partial}{}_{1}\langle\overline{\partial}{}\rangle^{s-\frac{1}{2}}\psi=\dfrac{-b_{2}^{+}f_{b}^{-}+b_{2}^{-}f_{b}^{+}}{b_{1}^{+}b_{2}^{-}-b_{1}^{-}b_{2}^{+}}\\
~\\
\overline{\partial}{}_{2}\langle\overline{\partial}{}\rangle^{s-\frac{1}{2}}\psi=\dfrac{b_{1}^{+}f_{b}^{-}-b_{1}^{-}f_{b}^{+}}{b_{1}^{+}b_{2}^{-}-b_{1}^{-}b_{2}^{+}}\end{cases}

with
